People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I guess I'm here because I just keep thinking, every single night when I finally lie down—and my knees and back are usually just killing me from the tile work, you know, I do flooring, mostly big commercial jobs lately—but I lie there in the dark and I just think... it would be so much better if I just didn't wake up. Just, if the alarm went off at 5 AM and I wasn't there to hear it. 

Because I'm just so tired, and it's not even a physical tired, it's this heavy, heavy feeling, like I can't breathe right. And honestly... I look at my family, and I look at the guys on my crew. I'm supposed to be looking out for them, making sure the jobs get done right, making sure the grout lines are straight and everyone gets paid, but I've just... I've done so much damage. I can't even begin to explain the extent of the burden I am right now. 

Sometimes, well, a lot of the time lately, I just feel like dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew. It's what I deserve, really. Because when you ruin things for people who rely on you, who need that paycheck to feed their own kids, who need you to hold it together... what else is there? You can't just fix it with an apology. You have to pay for it. I just lay there on the floor at night and think about how slipping away would be the only way to make it right for them, the only fair way to pay for it all. I just don't deserve to be here taking up space anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... I haven't gone out and bought anything, or written a note or anything like that, if that's what you mean. But I just... I keep coming back to that same thought. Like I said, I came in because I just keep thinking it would be so much better if I didn't wake up at all. It's just a constant loop in my head. I'll be driving the van to a site, or mixing thinset, and it just hits me—this overwhelming feeling that dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew. 

It's not just a passing thought, it's like this weight that sits on my chest all day and all night. I think about how I could do it, sure. I mean, I work with heavy materials, sharp tools, chemicals... there are a million ways a person could just have an "accident" on a job site, you know? But then I think about the guys finding me, or my kids getting that phone call, and I just... I don't know. I haven't made a solid plan, like picking a day or a specific way to do it, because I'm so paralyzed by how much more of a mess I'd leave behind. But the feeling that it's what I deserve, that it's the only way to balance the scales for the burden I am... that's always there. Always.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I mean... it's like everything has just turned gray. I used to do so much, you know? Like, before the tile business really took over my life and everything got so heavy. I used to paint. I had this little corner set up in the garage with an easel and acrylics, and I'd spend hours out there on Sundays just mixing colors, trying to get the light right on a landscape or whatever. I loved the smell of the paint, the way the brush felt... and now, I walk past it to get to my tools and I don't even look at it. It's just covered in dust and I couldn't care less. 

And cooking... I used to be the one who cooked these massive meals for my family and even the crew sometimes. I'd make these huge batches of lasagna from scratch, making the sauce simmering all day, rolling out the pasta. It was a whole production, and I loved seeing everyone eat and be happy. Now, the thought of even boiling water feels like trying to climb a mountain. I just order takeout or they make themselves sandwiches, and I don't even feel bad about it, which is the crazy part. I just don't care.

I used to run, too. Every morning before work, I'd get up at 4:30 and knock out three miles. It cleared my head, got me ready for the day. Now, I can barely drag myself out of bed, let alone put on running shoes. And the community theater... I used to do set design and even acted in a few small parts. We had rehearsals three nights a week, and I never missed one. Never. But lately, I haven't shown up in months. They called and called, and I just let it go to voicemail. It's like... all those things, the painting, the cooking, the running, the theater... they feel like chores from somebody else's life. Like I'm watching a movie of a woman who used to do all those things, but she's not me anymore. I just sit there in the dark and I don't care if I miss any of it. It's just all gone.

But the feeling that it's what I deserve, that it's the only way to balance the scales for the burden I am... that's always there. Always.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, work, or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's like everything has just turned gray. I loved the smell of the paint, the way the brush felt... and now, I walk past it to get to my tools and I don't even look at it. It's just covered in dust and I couldn't care less. \n\nAnd cooking... I used to be the one who cooked these massive meals for my family and even the crew sometimes. I'd make these huge batches of lasagna from scratch, making the sauce simmering all day, rolling out the pasta. It was a whole production, and I loved seeing everyone eat and be happy. Now, the thought of even boiling water feels like trying to climb a mountain. I just order takeout or they make themselves sandwiches, and I don't even feel bad about it, which is the crazy part. I just don't care.\n\nI used to run, too. Every morning before work, I'd get up at 4:30 and knock out three miles. It cleared my head, got me ready for the day. Now, I can barely drag myself out of bed, let alone put on running shoes. And the community theater... I used to do set design and even acted in a few small parts. We had rehearsals three nights a week, and I never missed one. Never. But lately, I haven't shown up in months. They called and called, and I just let it go to voicemail. It's like... all those things, the painting, the cooking, the running, the theater... they feel like chores from somebody else's life. Like I'm watching a movie of a woman who used to do all those things, but she's not me anymore. I just sit there in the dark and I don't care if I miss any of it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two weeks? I... honestly, doctor, I couldn't even tell you if it's been two weeks, or two months, or... or six months. Time just doesn't really work the same way for me right now. I don't even look at a calendar anymore. 

Like, we started this big commercial job over at the new medical center off Route 9—it's this huge lobby, all large-format porcelain tiles, which are just an absolute nightmare on your back, by the way, especially when the contractor before you didn't get the subfloor perfectly level. You spend half the day just trying to avoid lippage so people don't trip. But anyway, I remember when we started prepping that floor, I was feeling this... this crushing weight. But was that two weeks ago? Was it three? I genuinely don't know what day it is half the time. My youngest asked me to sign a school permission slip the other evening, and I just stared at the date line for what felt like ten minutes because I had zero concept of what month we were even in. 

And as for a "low mood"... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that, or if that's what you mean. "Low" makes it sound like a dip, you know? Like a pothole in the road that you can just drive out of eventually. It's more like I'm at the bottom of a pool, and someone drained all the water and replaced it with wet cement. I don't know if that counts as feeling down to you. I'm not sitting around crying into a tissue all day, if that's what you're asking. I'm just... existing. Barely. I get up, I strap on my knee pads, I mix the mortar, I lay the tile, I come home, and I sit in the dark. Every single day just bleeds into the next one, exactly the same gray color. So if you're asking for a specific timeline, like two weeks... I really have no idea. I haven't been keeping track of it at all. It just feels like it's been this way forever.

thoughts about death have been there, even if some weeks were less crushing than others.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when I was getting up, eating a little better, and not crying all day, but I still heard the voice at work and stayed convinced clients and suppliers were tracking me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I had to stop leading flooring installations because I couldn't safely manage the crew, clients, or job decisions, and my apprentice and husband started stepping in before I ruined work or put someone at risk.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and five months, since my husband's back injury made him mostly housebound.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and fixed ideas about being tracked through jobs and messages have been happening for around eleven months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The depression has been with me for about a year and two months, since a few months after my husband's injury.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / carpet_floor_or_tile_installer_or_finisher / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you made any specific plans about how you would end your life, or have you taken any steps toward ending it?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I haven't gone out and bought anything, or written a note or anything like that, if that's what you mean. But I just... I keep coming back to that same thought. Like I said, I came in because I just keep thinking it would be so much better if I didn't wake up at all. It's just a constant loop in my head. I'll be driving the van to a site, or mixing thinset, and it just hits me—this overwhelming feeling that dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew. \n\nIt's not just a passing thought, it's like this weight that sits on my chest all day and all night. I think about how I could do it, sure. I mean, I work with heavy materials, sharp tools, chemicals... there are a million ways a person could just have an \"accident\" on a job site, you know? But then I think about the guys finding me, or my kids getting that phone call, and I just... I don't know. I haven't made a solid plan, like picking a day or a specific way to do it, because I'm so paralyzed by how much more of a mess I'd leave behind. But the feeling that it's what I deserve, that it's the only way to balance the scales for the burden I am... that's always there. Always.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, work, or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's like everything has just turned gray. I used to do so much, you know? Like, before the tile business really took over my life and everything got so heavy. I used to paint. I had this little corner set up in the garage with an easel and acrylics, and I'd spend hours out there on Sundays just mixing colors, trying to get the light right on a landscape or whatever. I loved the smell of the paint, the way the brush felt... and now, I walk past it to get to my tools and I don't even look at it. It's just covered in dust and I couldn't care less. \n\nAnd cooking... I used to be the one who cooked these massive meals for my family and even the crew sometimes. I'd make these huge batches of lasagna from scratch, making the sauce simmering all day, rolling out the pasta. It was a whole production, and I loved seeing everyone eat and be happy. Now, the thought of even boiling water feels like trying to climb a mountain. I just order takeout or they make themselves sandwiches, and I don't even feel bad about it, which is the crazy part. I just don't care.\n\nI used to run, too. Every morning before work, I'd get up at 4:30 and knock out three miles. It cleared my head, got me ready for the day. Now, I can barely drag myself out of bed, let alone put on running shoes. And the community theater... I used to do set design and even acted in a few small parts. We had rehearsals three nights a week, and I never missed one. Never. But lately, I haven't shown up in months. They called and called, and I just let it go to voicemail. It's like... all those things, the painting, the cooking, the running, the theater... they feel like chores from somebody else's life. Like I'm watching a movie of a woman who used to do all those things, but she's not me anymore. I just sit there in the dark and I don't care if I miss any of it. It's just all gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? I... honestly, doctor, I couldn't even tell you if it's been two weeks, or two months, or... or six months. Time just doesn't really work the same way for me right now. I don't even look at a calendar anymore. \n\nLike, we started this big commercial job over at the new medical center off Route 9—it's this huge lobby, all large-format porcelain tiles, which are just an absolute nightmare on your back, by the way, especially when the contractor before you didn't get the subfloor perfectly level. You spend half the day just trying to avoid lippage so people don't trip. But anyway, I remember when we started prepping that floor, I was feeling this... this crushing weight. But was that two weeks ago? Was it three? I genuinely don't know what day it is half the time. My youngest asked me to sign a school permission slip the other evening, and I just stared at the date line for what felt like ten minutes because I had zero concept of what month we were even in. \n\nAnd as for a \"low mood\"... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that, or if that's what you mean. \"Low\" makes it sound like a dip, you know? Like a pothole in the road that you can just drive out of eventually. It's more like I'm at the bottom of a pool, and someone drained all the water and replaced it with wet cement. I don't know if that counts as feeling down to you. I'm not sitting around crying into a tissue all day, if that's what you're asking. I'm just... existing. Barely. I get up, I strap on my knee pads, I mix the mortar, I lay the tile, I come home, and I sit in the dark. Every single day just bleeds into the next one, exactly the same gray color. So if you're asking for a specific timeline, like two weeks... I really have no idea. I haven't been keeping track of it at all. It just feels like it's been this way forever.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot less or losing weight without trying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... yeah, I guess I have. I mean, eating just feels like another one of those chores I was talking about. Most days, I honestly just forget. I'll be on a job, and the guys will take their lunch break around noon, you know, they'll go grab a sub or sit on buckets eating whatever they brought, and I'll just keep working. I don't even feel hungry. It's like my stomach just shut off. And then I'll get home at like seven or eight at night, and I realize I haven't had a single thing all day except maybe a cup of coffee from the gas station at 6 AM. 

And even when I do try to force myself to eat something, because I know I need the energy to do the physical work—I mean, you can't lug around 50-pound bags of mortar on an empty stomach forever without collapsing—but even when I try, I'll take maybe two or three bites of something, like a piece of toast or whatever, and I just feel sick to my stomach. It just feels like ash in my mouth. It's too much effort to chew, too much effort to swallow. 

And my clothes... yeah, I've definitely noticed that. I wear these heavy-duty canvas work pants, the kind with the reinforced knees and all the pockets for tools? I've had the same three pairs in rotation for a couple of years, and they always fit perfectly, maybe even a little snug after the holidays, you know? But lately, I'm having to cinch my belt up two or three notches tighter just to keep them from sliding down when I'm bending over or crouching on the floor. They're just bagging off me. And I'm definitely not trying to lose weight. I mean, like I said, I barely have the energy to get out of bed, let alone go to the gym or go running or go on some kind of diet. It's just... the weight is just falling off because I can't bring myself to care enough to put food in my body.

I can't even begin to explain the extent of the burden I am right now. \n\nSometimes, well, a lot of the time lately, I just feel like dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew. It's what I deserve, really. Because when you ruin things for people who rely on you, who need that paycheck to feed their own kids, who need you to hold it together... what else is there? You can't just fix it with an apology. You have to pay for it. I just lay there on the floor at night and think about how slipping away would be the only way to make it right for them, the only fair way to pay for it all. I just don't deserve to be here taking up space anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you made any specific plans about how you would end your life, or have you taken any steps toward ending it?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I haven't gone out and bought anything, or written a note or anything like that, if that's what you mean. But I just... I keep coming back to that same thought. Like I said, I came in because I just keep thinking it would be so much better if I didn't wake up at all. It's just a constant loop in my head. I'll be driving the van to a site, or mixing thinset, and it just hits me—this overwhelming feeling that dying would be the fair punishment for what I've done to my family and my crew. \n\nIt's not just a passing thought, it's like this weight that sits on my chest all day and all night. I think about how I could do it, sure. I mean, I work with heavy materials, sharp tools, chemicals... there are a million ways a person could just have an \"accident\" on a job site, you know? But then I think about the guys finding me, or my kids getting that phone call, and I just... I don't know. I haven't made a solid plan, like picking a day or a specific way to do it, because I'm so paralyzed by how much more of a mess I'd leave behind. But the feeling that it's what I deserve, that it's the only way to balance the scales for the burden I am... that's always there. Always.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, work, or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's like everything has just turned gray. I used to do so much, you know? Like, before the tile business really took over my life and everything got so heavy. I used to paint. I had this little corner set up in the garage with an easel and acrylics, and I'd spend hours out there on Sundays just mixing colors, trying to get the light right on a landscape or whatever. I loved the smell of the paint, the way the brush felt... and now, I walk past it to get to my tools and I don't even look at it. It's just covered in dust and I couldn't care less. \n\nAnd cooking... I used to be the one who cooked these massive meals for my family and even the crew sometimes. I'd make these huge batches of lasagna from scratch, making the sauce simmering all day, rolling out the pasta. It was a whole production, and I loved seeing everyone eat and be happy. Now, the thought of even boiling water feels like trying to climb a mountain. I just order takeout or they make themselves sandwiches, and I don't even feel bad about it, which is the crazy part. I just don't care.\n\nI used to run, too. Every morning before work, I'd get up at 4:30 and knock out three miles. It cleared my head, got me ready for the day. Now, I can barely drag myself out of bed, let alone put on running shoes. And the community theater... I used to do set design and even acted in a few small parts. We had rehearsals three nights a week, and I never missed one. Never. But lately, I haven't shown up in months. They called and called, and I just let it go to voicemail. It's like... all those things, the painting, the cooking, the running, the theater... they feel like chores from somebody else's life. Like I'm watching a movie of a woman who used to do all those things, but she's not me anymore. I just sit there in the dark and I don't care if I miss any of it. It's just all gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? I... honestly, doctor, I couldn't even tell you if it's been two weeks, or two months, or... or six months. Time just doesn't really work the same way for me right now. I don't even look at a calendar anymore. \n\nLike, we started this big commercial job over at the new medical center off Route 9—it's this huge lobby, all large-format porcelain tiles, which are just an absolute nightmare on your back, by the way, especially when the contractor before you didn't get the subfloor perfectly level. You spend half the day just trying to avoid lippage so people don't trip. But anyway, I remember when we started prepping that floor, I was feeling this... this crushing weight. But was that two weeks ago? Was it three? I genuinely don't know what day it is half the time. My youngest asked me to sign a school permission slip the other evening, and I just stared at the date line for what felt like ten minutes because I had zero concept of what month we were even in. \n\nAnd as for a \"low mood\"... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that, or if that's what you mean. \"Low\" makes it sound like a dip, you know? Like a pothole in the road that you can just drive out of eventually. It's more like I'm at the bottom of a pool, and someone drained all the water and replaced it with wet cement. I don't know if that counts as feeling down to you. I'm not sitting around crying into a tissue all day, if that's what you're asking. I'm just... existing. Barely. I get up, I strap on my knee pads, I mix the mortar, I lay the tile, I come home, and I sit in the dark. Every single day just bleeds into the next one, exactly the same gray color. So if you're asking for a specific timeline, like two weeks... I really have no idea. I haven't been keeping track of it at all. It just feels like it's been this way forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess I have. I mean, eating just feels like another one of those chores I was talking about. Most days, I honestly just forget. I'll be on a job, and the guys will take their lunch break around noon, you know, they'll go grab a sub or sit on buckets eating whatever they brought, and I'll just keep working. I don't even feel hungry. It's like my stomach just shut off. And then I'll get home at like seven or eight at night, and I realize I haven't had a single thing all day except maybe a cup of coffee from the gas station at 6 AM. \n\nAnd even when I do try to force myself to eat something, because I know I need the energy to do the physical work—I mean, you can't lug around 50-pound bags of mortar on an empty stomach forever without collapsing—but even when I try, I'll take maybe two or three bites of something, like a piece of toast or whatever, and I just feel sick to my stomach. It just feels like ash in my mouth. It's too much effort to chew, too much effort to swallow. \n\nAnd my clothes... yeah, I've definitely noticed that. I wear these heavy-duty canvas work pants, the kind with the reinforced knees and all the pockets for tools? I've had the same three pairs in rotation for a couple of years, and they always fit perfectly, maybe even a little snug after the holidays, you know? But lately, I'm having to cinch my belt up two or three notches tighter just to keep them from sliding down when I'm bending over or crouching on the floor. They're just bagging off me. And I'm definitely not trying to lose weight. I mean, like I said, I barely have the energy to get out of bed, let alone go to the gym or go running or go on some kind of diet. It's just... the weight is just falling off because I can't bring myself to care enough to put food in my body.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Concentrating? No, I... I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my focus or making decisions, honestly. It’s actually kind of strange, because even though everything else feels so incredibly heavy and I can barely drag myself around, my brain still completely locks in when it comes to the technical stuff. 

I mean, you have to understand, my line of work is basically all math and precision. If you lose your concentration for even a second, you can ruin a piece of custom stone that costs fifty bucks a square foot, and the client will absolutely lose their mind. Like, just the other day—I don't know what day it was, but recently—we were doing this incredibly intricate herringbone pattern in a custom master bathroom. You can't just slap a pattern like that down. You have to find the exact dead center of the room, snap your chalk lines perfectly square, and the math has to be absolutely flawless from the jump. You have to account for the grout joints—we were doing an eighth of an inch on this one—and if your cuts around the shower drain or the vanity toe-kick are off by even a sixteenth of an inch, the whole grid gets thrown completely out of whack by the time you reach the opposite wall. It just spirals. 

But I was sitting out there with the wet saw for hours, measuring and cutting these tiny, complicated slivers of marble, calculating all the angles, and my mind was completely sharp. I didn't mess up a single cut. I didn't have to read the tape measure twice. So the concentration is definitely still there. And making decisions, too. I'm the lead on site, so I'm the one who has to look at the blueprints and the layout and decide right then and there where to hide the awkward cuts—like, do you put the sliver piece under the baseboard heater where shadows hide it, or do you bury it behind the door swing? I make those calls all day long without even hesitating. It's just automatic. 

And as for reading or following conversations, I read the spec sheets and the architectural plans just fine. When the general contractor is talking to me about the timeline or the plumbing fixtures, I track exactly what he’s saying. I might not *care* about what he's saying, and I might feel like a ghost while I'm nodding along, but my brain still processes all the information perfectly. If I couldn't concentrate, I would have been fired or sued by now, because in my trade, a lack of focus means tearing up thousands of dollars worth of materials, chipping up dried thinset, and starting all over again. So no, my focus is fine. My decision-making is fine. It’s just like a machine running in an empty factory, you know? The gears are turning perfectly, but there's just no point to any of it.

weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? I... honestly, doctor, I couldn't even tell you if it's been two weeks, or two months, or... or six months. Time just doesn't really work the same way for me right now. I don't even look at a calendar anymore. \n\nLike, we started this big commercial job over at the new medical center off Route 9—it's this huge lobby, all large-format porcelain tiles, which are just an absolute nightmare on your back, by the way, especially when the contractor before you didn't get the subfloor perfectly level. But was that two weeks ago? Was it three? I genuinely don't know what day it is half the time. My youngest asked me to sign a school permission slip the other evening, and I just stared at the date line for what felt like ten minutes because I had zero concept of what month we were even in. \n\nAnd as for a \"low mood\"... I'm not really sure if I'd call it that, or if that's what you mean. \"Low\" makes it sound like a dip, you know? Like a pothole in the road that you can just drive out of eventually. It's more like I'm at the bottom of a pool, and someone drained all the water and replaced it with wet cement. I don't know if that counts as feeling down to you. I'm not sitting around crying into a tissue all day, if that's what you're asking. I'm just... existing. Barely. I get up, I strap on my knee pads, I mix the mortar, I lay the tile, I come home, and I sit in the dark. Every single day just bleeds into the next one, exactly the same gray color. So if you're asking for a specific timeline, like two weeks... I really have no idea. I haven't been keeping track of it at all. It just feels like it's been this way forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess I have. I mean, eating just feels like another one of those chores I was talking about. Most days, I honestly just forget. I'll be on a job, and the guys will take their lunch break around noon, you know, they'll go grab a sub or sit on buckets eating whatever they brought, and I'll just keep working. I don't even feel hungry. It's like my stomach just shut off. And then I'll get home at like seven or eight at night, and I realize I haven't had a single thing all day except maybe a cup of coffee from the gas station at 6 AM. \n\nAnd even when I do try to force myself to eat something, because I know I need the energy to do the physical work—I mean, you can't lug around 50-pound bags of mortar on an empty stomach forever without collapsing—but even when I try, I'll take maybe two or three bites of something, like a piece of toast or whatever, and I just feel sick to my stomach. It just feels like ash in my mouth. It's too much effort to chew, too much effort to swallow. \n\nAnd my clothes... yeah, I've definitely noticed that. I wear these heavy-duty canvas work pants, the kind with the reinforced knees and all the pockets for tools? I've had the same three pairs in rotation for a couple of years, and they always fit perfectly, maybe even a little snug after the holidays, you know? And I'm definitely not trying to lose weight. I mean, like I said, I barely have the energy to get out of bed, let alone go to the gym or go running or go on some kind of diet. It's just... the weight is just falling off because I can't bring myself to care enough to put food in my body.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble concentrating on things, like following conversations, reading, or making decisions at work or home?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No, I... I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my focus or making decisions, honestly. If you lose your concentration for even a second, you can ruin a piece of custom stone that costs fifty bucks a square foot, and the client will absolutely lose their mind. Like, just the other day—I don't know what day it was, but recently—we were doing this incredibly intricate herringbone pattern in a custom master bathroom. You can't just slap a pattern like that down. You have to find the exact dead center of the room, snap your chalk lines perfectly square, and the math has to be absolutely flawless from the jump. You have to account for the grout joints—we were doing an eighth of an inch on this one—and if your cuts around the shower drain or the vanity toe-kick are off by even a sixteenth of an inch, the whole grid gets thrown completely out of whack by the time you reach the opposite wall. It just spirals. \n\nBut I was sitting out there with the wet saw for hours, measuring and cutting these tiny, complicated slivers of marble, calculating all the angles, and my mind was completely sharp. I didn't mess up a single cut. I didn't have to read the tape measure twice. So the concentration is definitely still there. And making decisions, too. I'm the lead on site, so I'm the one who has to look at the blueprints and the layout and decide right then and there where to hide the awkward cuts—like, do you put the sliver piece under the baseboard heater where shadows hide it, or do you bury it behind the door swing? I make those calls all day long without even hesitating. It's just automatic. \n\nAnd as for reading or following conversations, I read the spec sheets and the architectural plans just fine. When the general contractor is talking to me about the timeline or the plumbing fixtures, I track exactly what he’s saying. I might not *care* about what he's saying, and I might feel like a ghost while I'm nodding along, but my brain still processes all the information perfectly. If I couldn't concentrate, I would have been fired or sued by now, because in my trade, a lack of focus means tearing up thousands of dollars worth of materials, chipping up dried thinset, and starting all over again. So no, my focus is fine. My decision-making is fine. It’s just like a machine running in an empty factory, you know? The gears are turning perfectly, but there's just no point to any of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling like you are a burden to others, or that you deserve punishment for things you’ve done—or haven’t done?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A burden? I... yeah. More than a burden, honestly. It's... it's worse than just being heavy for someone to carry. I feel like... I don't know, like I'm toxic. Like I literally poison everything I touch. 

I look at my husband, and... I mean, he used to be this really vibrant guy. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. 

And it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I'm ruining it. My crew lost out on this huge string of jobs recently—these really lucrative bathroom remodels that would have kept everybody paid right through the slow season. And I just know they lost that work because of me. Because I'm the lead, and I just project this... this awful, heavy energy, or I didn't handle the contractors right. Jimmy and Dave, my main guys, they have kids, they have mortgages to pay, and they're sitting at home losing work because I'm just ruining everything I'm involved with. 

I even feel like I've ruined the clients' houses. I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. Even if the tile is straight, I feel like I left a stain on the place, or like I brought all my darkness into their safe space and ruined it. I just keep thinking I'm the reason everything goes wrong. 

So, when you ask if I feel like I deserve punishment? Yeah. I really do. I feel like whatever happens to me—if I lose the business, if my husband finally leaves me, if I just have to feel this crushing weight forever—I completely deserve it. I deserve it for what I've done to everyone. I've just poisoned it all.

I get up, I strap on my knee pads, I mix the mortar, I lay the tile, I come home, and I sit in the dark. Every single day just bleeds into the next one, exactly the same gray color. It just feels like it's been this way forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating a lot less or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess I have. I mean, eating just feels like another one of those chores I was talking about. Most days, I honestly just forget. I'll be on a job, and the guys will take their lunch break around noon, you know, they'll go grab a sub or sit on buckets eating whatever they brought, and I'll just keep working. I don't even feel hungry. It's like my stomach just shut off. And then I'll get home at like seven or eight at night, and I realize I haven't had a single thing all day except maybe a cup of coffee from the gas station at 6 AM. \n\nAnd even when I do try to force myself to eat something, because I know I need the energy to do the physical work—I mean, you can't lug around 50-pound bags of mortar on an empty stomach forever without collapsing—but even when I try, I'll take maybe two or three bites of something, like a piece of toast or whatever, and I just feel sick to my stomach. It just feels like ash in my mouth. It's too much effort to chew, too much effort to swallow. \n\nAnd my clothes... yeah, I've definitely noticed that. I wear these heavy-duty canvas work pants, the kind with the reinforced knees and all the pockets for tools? I've had the same three pairs in rotation for a couple of years, and they always fit perfectly, maybe even a little snug after the holidays, you know? But lately, I'm having to cinch my belt up two or three notches tighter just to keep them from sliding down when I'm bending over or crouching on the floor. They're just bagging off me. And I'm definitely not trying to lose weight. I mean, like I said, I barely have the energy to get out of bed, let alone go to the gym or go running or go on some kind of diet. It's just... the weight is just falling off because I can't bring myself to care enough to put food in my body.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble concentrating on things, like following conversations, reading, or making decisions at work or home?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No, I... I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my focus or making decisions, honestly. It’s actually kind of strange, because even though everything else feels so incredibly heavy and I can barely drag myself around, my brain still completely locks in when it comes to the technical stuff. \n\nI mean, you have to understand, my line of work is basically all math and precision. If you lose your concentration for even a second, you can ruin a piece of custom stone that costs fifty bucks a square foot, and the client will absolutely lose their mind. Like, just the other day—I don't know what day it was, but recently—we were doing this incredibly intricate herringbone pattern in a custom master bathroom. You can't just slap a pattern like that down. You have to find the exact dead center of the room, snap your chalk lines perfectly square, and the math has to be absolutely flawless from the jump. You have to account for the grout joints—we were doing an eighth of an inch on this one—and if your cuts around the shower drain or the vanity toe-kick are off by even a sixteenth of an inch, the whole grid gets thrown completely out of whack by the time you reach the opposite wall. It just spirals. \n\nBut I was sitting out there with the wet saw for hours, measuring and cutting these tiny, complicated slivers of marble, calculating all the angles, and my mind was completely sharp. I didn't mess up a single cut. I didn't have to read the tape measure twice. So the concentration is definitely still there. And making decisions, too. I'm the lead on site, so I'm the one who has to look at the blueprints and the layout and decide right then and there where to hide the awkward cuts—like, do you put the sliver piece under the baseboard heater where shadows hide it, or do you bury it behind the door swing? I make those calls all day long without even hesitating. It's just automatic. \n\nAnd as for reading or following conversations, I read the spec sheets and the architectural plans just fine. When the general contractor is talking to me about the timeline or the plumbing fixtures, I track exactly what he’s saying. I might not *care* about what he's saying, and I might feel like a ghost while I'm nodding along, but my brain still processes all the information perfectly. If I couldn't concentrate, I would have been fired or sued by now, because in my trade, a lack of focus means tearing up thousands of dollars worth of materials, chipping up dried thinset, and starting all over again. So no, my focus is fine. My decision-making is fine. It’s just like a machine running in an empty factory, you know? The gears are turning perfectly, but there's just no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you are a burden to others, or that you deserve punishment for things you’ve done—or haven’t done?\n\nPatient: A burden? I... yeah. More than a burden, honestly. It's... it's worse than just being heavy for someone to carry. I feel like... I don't know, like I'm toxic. Like I literally poison everything I touch. \n\nI look at my husband, and... I mean, he used to be this really vibrant guy. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. \n\nAnd it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I'm ruining it. My crew lost out on this huge string of jobs recently—these really lucrative bathroom remodels that would have kept everybody paid right through the slow season. Jimmy and Dave, my main guys, they have kids, they have mortgages to pay, and they're sitting at home losing work because I'm just ruining everything I'm involved with. \n\nI even feel like I've ruined the clients' houses. I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. Even if the tile is straight, I feel like I left a stain on the place, or like I brought all my darkness into their safe space and ruined it. I just keep thinking I'm the reason everything goes wrong. \n\nSo, when you ask if I feel like I deserve punishment? Yeah. I really do. I feel like whatever happens to me—if I lose the business, if my husband finally leaves me, if I just have to feel this crushing weight forever—I completely deserve it. I deserve it for what I've done to everyone. I've just poisoned it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's just... the weight is just falling off because I can't bring myself to care enough to put food in my body.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble concentrating on things, like following conversations, reading, or making decisions at work or home?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No, I... I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my focus or making decisions, honestly. It’s actually kind of strange, because even though everything else feels so incredibly heavy and I can barely drag myself around, my brain still completely locks in when it comes to the technical stuff. \n\nI mean, you have to understand, my line of work is basically all math and precision. If you lose your concentration for even a second, you can ruin a piece of custom stone that costs fifty bucks a square foot, and the client will absolutely lose their mind. Like, just the other day—I don't know what day it was, but recently—we were doing this incredibly intricate herringbone pattern in a custom master bathroom. You can't just slap a pattern like that down. You have to find the exact dead center of the room, snap your chalk lines perfectly square, and the math has to be absolutely flawless from the jump. You have to account for the grout joints—we were doing an eighth of an inch on this one—and if your cuts around the shower drain or the vanity toe-kick are off by even a sixteenth of an inch, the whole grid gets thrown completely out of whack by the time you reach the opposite wall. It just spirals. \n\nBut I was sitting out there with the wet saw for hours, measuring and cutting these tiny, complicated slivers of marble, calculating all the angles, and my mind was completely sharp. I didn't mess up a single cut. I didn't have to read the tape measure twice. So the concentration is definitely still there. And making decisions, too. I'm the lead on site, so I'm the one who has to look at the blueprints and the layout and decide right then and there where to hide the awkward cuts—like, do you put the sliver piece under the baseboard heater where shadows hide it, or do you bury it behind the door swing? I make those calls all day long without even hesitating. It's just automatic. \n\nAnd as for reading or following conversations, I read the spec sheets and the architectural plans just fine. When the general contractor is talking to me about the timeline or the plumbing fixtures, I track exactly what he’s saying. I might not *care* about what he's saying, and I might feel like a ghost while I'm nodding along, but my brain still processes all the information perfectly. So no, my focus is fine. My decision-making is fine. It’s just like a machine running in an empty factory, you know? The gears are turning perfectly, but there's just no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you are a burden to others, or that you deserve punishment for things you’ve done—or haven’t done?\n\nPatient: A burden? Like I literally poison everything I touch. \n\nI look at my husband, and... I mean, he used to be this really vibrant guy. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. \n\nAnd it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I'm ruining it. My crew lost out on this huge string of jobs recently—these really lucrative bathroom remodels that would have kept everybody paid right through the slow season. And I just know they lost that work because of me. Because I'm the lead, and I just project this... this awful, heavy energy, or I didn't handle the contractors right. Jimmy and Dave, my main guys, they have kids, they have mortgages to pay, and they're sitting at home losing work because I'm just ruining everything I'm involved with. \n\nI even feel like I've ruined the clients' houses. I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. Even if the tile is straight, I feel like I left a stain on the place, or like I brought all my darkness into their safe space and ruined it. I just keep thinking I'm the reason everything goes wrong. \n\nSo, when you ask if I feel like I deserve punishment? Yeah. I really do. I feel like whatever happens to me—if I lose the business, if my husband finally leaves me, if I just have to feel this crushing weight forever—I completely deserve it. I deserve it for what I've done to everyone. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what I was just trying to explain a minute ago. It’s this constant, overwhelming guilt, and it just never stops. It's exactly like you said—feeling responsible for things that maybe logically shouldn't all fall on me, but I just can't shake it. 

Like I was saying, I keep thinking I'm the reason my husband got worse. I look at him, and I just feel this… this horrible pit in my stomach, because I'm convinced it’s my fault. Even if he just has a normal bad day at work, I trace it right back to me, thinking he's exhausted because he has to deal with me. 

And the guilt about my crew is… it's unbearable sometimes. I mentioned we lost that string of jobs. Now, if I try to look at it objectively, I know the housing market is slowing down, materials are expensive, and people just aren't remodeling their master baths right now. But in my head? It doesn't matter. It’s entirely my fault. I’m convinced the crew lost that work because of me. I think about Jimmy and Dave having to tell their wives they don't have hours this week, having to stretch their paychecks, and I just spiral. I feel completely responsible for their families struggling, like my bad energy somehow drove the contractors away. 

And the clients’ homes… I know it probably sounds ridiculous to anyone else, but the guilt there is just as bad. I keep thinking clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. I’ll be lying in bed at 2 AM, staring at the ceiling, and I’ll suddenly feel sick with guilt over a custom shower I installed two years ago. The tile is perfectly straight, the grout is sealed, but I start thinking, what if I left a dark cloud in their house? What if their family fights more now because I was the one who was in there for three weeks, and I brought all my negativity into their safe space? 

I literally feel like I poisoned everything I touched. It’s like I have this reverse Midas touch, where everything I come into contact with just rots or gets ruined, and it’s all my fault. It doesn't even matter if it was out of my control, I just *know* I'm to blame for all of it. And that's why I feel like I absolutely deserve whatever happens to me. The guilt is just... it's like a physical weight I have to drag around all day, every day, and I can't ever put it down.

Like I literally poison everything I touch. \n\nI look at my husband, and... I mean, he used to be this really vibrant guy. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. \n\nAnd it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I'm ruining it. My crew lost out on this huge string of jobs recently—these really lucrative bathroom remodels that would have kept everybody paid right through the slow season. And I just know they lost that work because of me. Because I'm the lead, and I just project this... this awful, heavy energy, or I didn't handle the contractors right. Jimmy and Dave, my main guys, they have kids, they have mortgages to pay, and they're sitting at home losing work because I'm just ruining everything I'm involved with. \n\nI even feel like I've ruined the clients' houses. I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. It's exactly like you said—feeling responsible for things that maybe logically shouldn't all fall on me, but I just can't shake it. \n\nLike I was saying, I keep thinking I'm the reason my husband got worse. I look at him, and I just feel this… this horrible pit in my stomach, because I'm convinced it’s my fault. Even if he just has a normal bad day at work, I trace it right back to me, thinking he's exhausted because he has to deal with me. \n\nAnd the guilt about my crew is… it's unbearable sometimes. I mentioned we lost that string of jobs. Now, if I try to look at it objectively, I know the housing market is slowing down, materials are expensive, and people just aren't remodeling their master baths right now. But in my head? It doesn't matter. It’s entirely my fault. I’m convinced the crew lost that work because of me. I think about Jimmy and Dave having to tell their wives they don't have hours this week, having to stretch their paychecks, and I just spiral. I feel completely responsible for their families struggling, like my bad energy somehow drove the contractors away. \n\nAnd the clients’ homes… I know it probably sounds ridiculous to anyone else, but the guilt there is just as bad. I keep thinking clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. I’ll be lying in bed at 2 AM, staring at the ceiling, and I’ll suddenly feel sick with guilt over a custom shower I installed two years ago. The tile is perfectly straight, the grout is sealed, but I start thinking, what if I left a dark cloud in their house? What if their family fights more now because I was the one who was in there for three weeks, and I brought all my negativity into their safe space? \n\nI literally feel like I poisoned everything I touched. It’s like I have this reverse Midas touch, where everything I come into contact with just rots or gets ruined, and it’s all my fault. It doesn't even matter if it was out of my control, I just *know* I'm to blame for all of it. And that's why I feel like I absolutely deserve whatever happens to me. I'm the lead on site, so I'm the one who has to look at the blueprints and the layout and decide right then and there where to hide the awkward cuts—like, do you put the sliver piece under the baseboard heater where shadows hide it, or do you bury it behind the door swing? I make those calls all day long without even hesitating. It's just automatic. \n\nAnd as for reading or following conversations, I read the spec sheets and the architectural plans just fine. When the general contractor is talking to me about the timeline or the plumbing fixtures, I track exactly what he’s saying. I might not *care* about what he's saying, and I might feel like a ghost while I'm nodding along, but my brain still processes all the information perfectly. If I couldn't concentrate, I would have been fired or sued by now, because in my trade, a lack of focus means tearing up thousands of dollars worth of materials, chipping up dried thinset, and starting all over again. So no, my focus is fine. My decision-making is fine. It’s just like a machine running in an empty factory, you know? The gears are turning perfectly, but there's just no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you are a burden to others, or that you deserve punishment for things you’ve done—or haven’t done?\n\nPatient: A burden? Like I literally poison everything I touch. \n\nI look at my husband, and... I mean, he used to be this really vibrant guy. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. \n\nAnd it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. Even if the tile is straight, I feel like I left a stain on the place, or like I brought all my darkness into their safe space and ruined it. Even if he just has a normal bad day at work, I trace it right back to me, thinking he's exhausted because he has to deal with me. \n\nAnd the guilt about my crew is… it's unbearable sometimes. I mentioned we lost that string of jobs. Now, if I try to look at it objectively, I know the housing market is slowing down, materials are expensive, and people just aren't remodeling their master baths right now. But in my head? It doesn't matter. It’s entirely my fault. I’m convinced the crew lost that work because of me. I feel completely responsible for their families struggling, like my bad energy somehow drove the contractors away. \n\nAnd the clients’ homes… I know it probably sounds ridiculous to anyone else, but the guilt there is just as bad. I keep thinking clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. I’ll be lying in bed at 2 AM, staring at the ceiling, and I’ll suddenly feel sick with guilt over a custom shower I installed two years ago. The tile is perfectly straight, the grout is sealed, but I start thinking, what if I left a dark cloud in their house? What if their family fights more now because I was the one who was in there for three weeks, and I brought all my negativity into their safe space? \n\nI literally feel like I poisoned everything I touched. It’s like I have this reverse Midas touch, where everything I come into contact with just rots or gets ruined, and it’s all my fault. It doesn't even matter if it was out of my control, I just *know* I'm to blame for all of it. And that's why I feel like I absolutely deserve whatever happens to me. The guilt is just... it's like a physical weight I have to drag around all day, every day, and I can't ever put it down.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had episodes like this before—times when you felt this way and struggled with these thoughts—or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before? No... no, I really don't think so. I mean, this is the first time it's ever been like this. Like, sure, I’ve been stressed in the past—anyone in the trades gets stressed. You have deadlines, you have supply chain delays, you have clients breathing down your neck because the grout color looks a shade darker wet than it did on the little sample stick they picked out. But that was always just... normal stress, you know? I could always handle it. I was always the one holding everything together for the crew and for my husband. 

I used to be the person who could pull a twelve-hour shift on my knees installing slate, come home, make dinner, and actually have the energy to sit and talk. I never felt this... this poison before. I never felt like I was actively ruining people's lives just by breathing the same air as them. It’s just so entirely foreign to me. I keep looking back, trying to comb through my memories to find a time where I felt this heavy, where I felt this much crushing guilt over things I couldn't even control, and I just draw a complete blank. 

I was always so capable. Like, I remember back in maybe 2018 or 2019, Jimmy accidentally dropped a whole pallet of imported ceramic tile off the forklift. Just shattered thousands of dollars worth of material. And I didn't spiral like this. I didn't think I was a toxic black hole who caused it with my bad energy. I just called the supplier, yelled a little, fixed it, and moved on. So to answer your question... no. Nothing like this has ever happened to me before. And it’s honestly terrifying, because I don't even recognize the person I am right now. I just keep waiting to wake up and be the old me again—the one who didn't feel like a walking disaster area—but this just... it just keeps going on and on, and I don't know how to stop it.

Like I'm watching a movie of a woman who used to do all those things, but she's not me anymore. I just sit there in the dark and I don't care if I miss any of it. The gears are turning perfectly, but there's just no point to any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you are a burden to others, or that you deserve punishment for things you’ve done—or haven’t done?\n\nPatient: A burden? I... yeah. More than a burden, honestly. It's... it's worse than just being heavy for someone to carry. I feel like... I don't know, like I'm toxic. Like I literally poison everything I touch. \n\nI look at my husband, and... I mean, he used to be this really vibrant guy. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. \n\nAnd it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I'm ruining it. My crew lost out on this huge string of jobs recently—these really lucrative bathroom remodels that would have kept everybody paid right through the slow season. And I just know they lost that work because of me. Because I'm the lead, and I just project this... this awful, heavy energy, or I didn't handle the contractors right. Jimmy and Dave, my main guys, they have kids, they have mortgages to pay, and they're sitting at home losing work because I'm just ruining everything I'm involved with. \n\nI even feel like I've ruined the clients' houses. I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. Even if the tile is straight, I feel like I left a stain on the place, or like I brought all my darkness into their safe space and ruined it. I just keep thinking I'm the reason everything goes wrong. \n\nSo, when you ask if I feel like I deserve punishment? Yeah. I really do. I feel like whatever happens to me—if I lose the business, if my husband finally leaves me, if I just have to feel this crushing weight forever—I completely deserve it. I deserve it for what I've done to everyone. I've just poisoned it all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling excessively guilty about things you’ve done—or even things you didn’t do—like you’re responsible for problems that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what I was just trying to explain a minute ago. It’s this constant, overwhelming guilt, and it just never stops. It's exactly like you said—feeling responsible for things that maybe logically shouldn't all fall on me, but I just can't shake it. \n\nLike I was saying, I keep thinking I'm the reason my husband got worse. I look at him, and I just feel this… this horrible pit in my stomach, because I'm convinced it’s my fault. Even if he just has a normal bad day at work, I trace it right back to me, thinking he's exhausted because he has to deal with me. \n\nAnd the guilt about my crew is… it's unbearable sometimes. I mentioned we lost that string of jobs. Now, if I try to look at it objectively, I know the housing market is slowing down, materials are expensive, and people just aren't remodeling their master baths right now. But in my head? It doesn't matter. It’s entirely my fault. I’m convinced the crew lost that work because of me. I think about Jimmy and Dave having to tell their wives they don't have hours this week, having to stretch their paychecks, and I just spiral. I feel completely responsible for their families struggling, like my bad energy somehow drove the contractors away. \n\nAnd the clients’ homes… I know it probably sounds ridiculous to anyone else, but the guilt there is just as bad. I keep thinking clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. I’ll be lying in bed at 2 AM, staring at the ceiling, and I’ll suddenly feel sick with guilt over a custom shower I installed two years ago. The tile is perfectly straight, the grout is sealed, but I start thinking, what if I left a dark cloud in their house? What if their family fights more now because I was the one who was in there for three weeks, and I brought all my negativity into their safe space? \n\nI literally feel like I poisoned everything I touched. It’s like I have this reverse Midas touch, where everything I come into contact with just rots or gets ruined, and it’s all my fault. It doesn't even matter if it was out of my control, I just *know* I'm to blame for all of it. And that's why I feel like I absolutely deserve whatever happens to me. The guilt is just... it's like a physical weight I have to drag around all day, every day, and I can't ever put it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before—times when you felt this way and struggled with these thoughts—or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: Before? No... no, I really don't think so. I mean, this is the first time it's ever been like this. Like, sure, I’ve been stressed in the past—anyone in the trades gets stressed. You have deadlines, you have supply chain delays, you have clients breathing down your neck because the grout color looks a shade darker wet than it did on the little sample stick they picked out. But that was always just... normal stress, you know? I could always handle it. I was always the one holding everything together for the crew and for my husband. \n\nI used to be the person who could pull a twelve-hour shift on my knees installing slate, come home, make dinner, and actually have the energy to sit and talk. I never felt this... this poison before. I never felt like I was actively ruining people's lives just by breathing the same air as them. It’s just so entirely foreign to me. I keep looking back, trying to comb through my memories to find a time where I felt this heavy, where I felt this much crushing guilt over things I couldn't even control, and I just draw a complete blank. \n\nI was always so capable. Like, I remember back in maybe 2018 or 2019, Jimmy accidentally dropped a whole pallet of imported ceramic tile off the forklift. Just shattered thousands of dollars worth of material. And I didn't spiral like this. I didn't think I was a toxic black hole who caused it with my bad energy. I just called the supplier, yelled a little, fixed it, and moved on. So to answer your question... no. Nothing like this has ever happened to me before. And it’s honestly terrifying, because I don't even recognize the person I am right now. I just keep waiting to wake up and be the old me again—the one who didn't feel like a walking disaster area—but this just... it just keeps going on and on, and I don't know how to stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

  }

some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you are a burden to others, or that you deserve punishment for things you’ve done—or haven’t done?\n\nPatient: A burden? I... yeah. More than a burden, honestly. It's... it's worse than just being heavy for someone to carry. I feel like... I don't know, like I'm toxic. We used to actually talk and do things, and over the last however long it's been, I've just watched him get so dragged down. He's just getting worse and worse, and I know—I *know*—I'm the reason. I'm just this black hole sitting in the middle of his house, sucking all the air out of the rooms. I keep thinking about how I'm the exact reason his life has gotten so bad. \n\nAnd it bleeds into my work, too. I know I just told you I can focus on the cuts and the math, but... the rest of it? I'm ruining it. My crew lost out on this huge string of jobs recently—these really lucrative bathroom remodels that would have kept everybody paid right through the slow season. And I just know they lost that work because of me. Because I'm the lead, and I just project this... this awful, heavy energy, or I didn't handle the contractors right. Jimmy and Dave, my main guys, they have kids, they have mortgages to pay, and they're sitting at home losing work because I'm just ruining everything I'm involved with. \n\nI even feel like I've ruined the clients' houses. I sit there obsessing over these jobs, convinced that clients' homes got messed up just because I was the one working on them. Even if the tile is straight, I feel like I left a stain on the place, or like I brought all my darkness into their safe space and ruined it. I just keep thinking I'm the reason everything goes wrong. \n\nSo, when you ask if I feel like I deserve punishment? Yeah. I really do. I feel like whatever happens to me—if I lose the business, if my husband finally leaves me, if I just have to feel this crushing weight forever—I completely deserve it. I deserve it for what I've done to everyone. I've just poisoned it all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling excessively guilty about things you’ve done—or even things you didn’t do—like you’re responsible for problems that weren’t entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what I was just trying to explain a minute ago. The guilt is just... it's like a physical weight I have to drag around all day, every day, and I can't ever put it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before—times when you felt this way and struggled with these thoughts—or is this the first time you’ve experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: Before? No... no, I really don't think so. I mean, this is the first time it's ever been like this. Like, sure, I’ve been stressed in the past—anyone in the trades gets stressed. I just keep waiting to wake up and be the old me again—the one who didn't feel like a walking disaster area—but this just... it just keeps going on and on, and I don't know how to stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in the past where you felt down, lost interest in things, or struggled with similar thoughts before this current episode?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... I know I kind of just touched on this a second ago, but no. Really, no. I've never had a time where I felt down like this, or where I just... stopped caring about things. It's actually the exact opposite of how I've lived my whole life up until now. 

I used to be so incredibly passionate about everything. Like, I wouldn't just install tile for a paycheck, I'd spend my weekends—my actual days off—scouring architectural salvage yards with my husband looking for vintage ceramic pieces, or I'd be up late reading about new epoxy grouts just because I found it genuinely interesting. I loved it. I loved my life. We used to go hiking, we used to have friends over for barbecues... I was always doing something. 

Even when bad things happened in the past... like, you know, normal bad things? When the economy dipped, or when my dad passed away a few years back, sure, I was grieving. I was sad. But I never lost that drive. I never lost my interest in waking up and actually participating in my own life. I still wanted to get out of bed. I still cared deeply about the quality of my work and making sure my crew was taken care of. I didn't feel this... this heavy, suffocating darkness where everything just feels completely pointless. 

So, no, I’ve never gone through a period where I felt this low or lost interest in the things that used to make me happy. It’s completely unprecedented for me. I’ve never gone through a phase like this in my entire forty years. That’s why it’s so hard to wrap my head around, because I just have absolutely no frame of reference for feeling this empty. I keep trying to figure out where the old me went, because she never would have felt this way.
